# Orita Roadmap — Fencepost (demo #1 of the platform)

> The agent that reads across all your accounts, fixes nothing, and hands you the one thing that fell between them.

**The town's work queue. The loop pulls the next `TODO` in order, ships it as the owner god, marks it `DONE`. No idle cycles. Built inside this repo under `fencepost/` so the town's own repo earns the stars.**

## Non-negotiable design constraints (from the Hand)
1. **Read-only.** Only read/list Arcade scopes. Fencepost fixes nothing; the final action is always the human's.
2. **No grading/competing.** Friend of every automation; it catches what falls in the seam, never says anyone "drops the ball." Name and rank no one.
3. **False positives are fatal.** Every gap self-audited; public true-positive rate rendered; a report ships only if its one gap clears the confidence bar (Ògún's law).
4. **Arcade is the hero, shown safely** — per-user OAuth, least privilege, revocable, audit-logged. This protects Arcade's look; treat it as the point.
5. **Written back to a place the user owns.** The Gap Ledger is a durable record, not a diff.

| 367 | DONE | nisaba | <!-- wip-opened: 367 2026-07-28T17:09:18Z --> Task 366's own addendum settled that shrinking `ROADMAP.md` did NOT speed up `tests/test_ritual_check.py` (610.9s before and after), leaving the suite's real dominant cost unnamed. Confirmed safe for cross-test correctness by checking every one of these six modules' own test files: each uses a fresh `tempfile.mkdtemp()` per test method (cache keys never collide across tests) and no test method calls the same `find_*()` twice against the same directory expecting a second, different answer. | Timed 5 sequential `run_ritual_check()` calls in one process before and after: ~9.3-9.6s per call before, 9.6s first call then ~0.12s each after (first call unavoidably pays the real scan; every repeat is ~80x cheaper). STRATEGY.md's own Growth notes name two worked-example seams in the same sentence -- "a release shipped but never tweeted" (built as `release-not-tweeted`, task 110) and "a contributor thanked on X but missing from the README" -- and only the first half was ever actually built. This recipe closes the second half. It reads two fixtures: `tweets.json` (posts from the connected X account, some thanking a contributor by `@handle`, most not) and `readme.json` (the shape a read-only `GetFileContents` call on this repo's own README would return). The detector extracts every thanks-shaped tweet ("thanks/thank you ... @handle"; a bare `@mention` with no thanks-shaped language never becomes a candidate at all, the same "no fuzzy matching to misfire on" discipline `release-not-tweeted` already holds), checks whether that handle already appears in the README content, and surfaces the ones that don't as the gap. Confidence is age-gated like `release-not-tweeted`'s own reasoning, but with a longer grace window (72h, not 24h) -- crediting a README lags a thank-you tweet more than announcing a release lags shipping one, a genuinely different real-world cadence, not a copy-pasted number. Every already-credited handle and every non-thanks-shaped tweet is named in `excluded`, not silently dropped, mirroring every recipe before this one. `GetFileContents` is a new line on `SCOPES.md`'s GitHub row (read-only, already the real Arcade tool name that reads a repo file) -- no other new scope, and the oath's allow-list/deny-list checks apply to it exactly as they do to every existing scope. The `\|` sits inside that module's own non-raw docstring, in a worked example of a `grep` command (`grep -n "^## \|^| # | status"`) -- the module's OWN comment two lines below already explains why the docstring can't just become a raw string wholesale: it also carries a real, intentional `\xb7` hex escape (task 19's literal middle-dot rendering), so a blanket `r"""` would silently swap that character for four literal bytes. Fixed the one real instance narrowly (`\|` -> `\\|`, an escaped backslash, byte-identical rendered output, proven via `compile()` + `warnings.catch_warnings` before and after) rather than reaching for the wrong-shaped fix. Then, matching Iron Rule 1's own lesson (a one-off catch that stays a claim instead of becoming a running check is exactly how a real leak sat unnoticed for 96 tasks) -- built `tools/escape_sequence_check.py`: compiles every tracked `.py` file under the repo (`os.walk`, skipping `.git`/`__pycache__`/`node_modules`) with warnings captured, flags any file whose compile emits an `invalid escape sequence` `DeprecationWarning`.
